Language / Idioma: English | Português

1. Introduction

Kernel-based prediction requires genomic, phenomic, environmental, and meteorological information to be expressed as compatible similarity matrices. This tutorial constructs these matrices from the original observations and explains how their dimensions, labels, and ordering are aligned for multi-environment prediction.

The script integrates four data layers:

  1. Phenotypic records (Pheno): grain yield (GY) and kernel weight (KW) by genotype and environment.
  2. Genomic markers (Geno): SNP markers processed in numerical format.
  3. NIR spectra (NIR): phenomic measurements used to build a spectral similarity kernel.
  4. Weather-derived covariates (W): meteorological descriptors available from the climate preprocessing step.

The resulting matrices represent distinct sources of similarity:

  • Linear kernels: ZG, ZP, ZEZE, ZW
  • Interaction kernels: ZGZE, ZPZE, ZGZW, ZPZW
  • Nonlinear Gaussian kernels: GGK, PGK, GGKE, PGKE, GGKW, PGKW
  • Nonlinear arccosine kernels: GAK, PAK, GAKE, PAKE, GAKW, PAKW

In the 18-model specification, Eta1-Eta9 combine the categorical environment effect with genomic and/or phenomic information, whereas Eta10-Eta18 also incorporate weather-derived similarity and related interactions. The distinction separates categorical environments from measured meteorological conditions.

Linear kernels and their interactions are shared between traits. Gaussian and arccosine kernels, including their interactions, are stored with _GY and _KW suffixes (12 per trait). In the GY construction examples, in-memory objects retain their unsuffixed names; KW objects use _KW.

All kernels must have the same observation order in their rows and columns. Otherwise, an entry in one matrix could refer to a different observation in another, making their joint use in a prediction model invalid.

Inputs, outputs and next step

Inputs of this step - phenotypic, genomic, and phenomic data - climate-derived covariates available in output/climate_results/

Outputs of this step - aligned observational objects - environment and weather design structures - linear kernels and their interactions, shared between GY and KW - 12 nonlinear and interaction kernels per trait, with _GY and _KW suffixes

Next step - the saved kernel objects are used by the variance-component and prediction-analysis stages.

2. R environment and dependencies

We first load the packages required for data handling, kernel construction, visualization, and memory management.

  • tidyverse: data wrangling and plotting.
  • data.table: fast reading of large files.
  • BGLR: downstream Bayesian genomic prediction framework.
  • parallel and doParallel: support for computationally intensive tasks.
  • MASS: additional statistical utilities.
  • patchwork: multi-panel figure composition for diagnostic plots.

The packages below support matrix construction, data wrangling, kernel calculations, optimization, and plotting.

suppressPackageStartupMessages({
  library(tidyverse)
  library(data.table)
  library(BGLR)
  library(parallel)
  library(doParallel)
  library(MASS)
  library(gridExtra)
  library(grid)
  library(knitr)
  library(kableExtra)
  library(tools)
  library(patchwork)
})

dir.create("output/Matrizes", recursive = TRUE, showWarnings = FALSE)
dir.create("output/Matrizes/figures", recursive = TRUE, showWarnings = FALSE)

3. Load the raw data

The construction and calibration chunks document the complete procedure and remain available for explicit execution. Normal rendering loads the persisted matrices instead, so the diagnostics and figures do not recalibrate kernels or overwrite RDS files.

The next section reads the raw inputs used in the kernel-construction pipeline. At this stage, the goal is only to import the data and inspect the basic structure of each source.

3.1 Load NIR records and phenotypes

The NIR.csv file contains identifiers, environments, phenotypic traits, and the spectral bands used later to build the phenomic kernel.

The code below reads the NIR data and prepares the phenomic input that will later be matched to the phenotypic and genomic records.

NIR <- fread("data/NIR.csv") %>% as.data.frame()

head(NIR[, 1:7])
unique(NIR$Env)

3.2 Load genomic markers

The genotype file contains one row per genotype and one column per marker. The first column (taxa) is the genotype identifier used to align genomic and phenotypic information.

The code below reads the genomic marker data that provide the basis for the genomic kernels used in the downstream models.

Geno <- fread("data/GAPIT.Genotype.Numerical.txt") %>% as.data.frame()

head(Geno[, 1:5])

3.3 Build the phenotype table used downstream

The modeling scripts later require a clean phenotype table containing only the identifiers and the target traits. We extract those columns directly from the NIR file to guarantee that the phenotype table remains synchronized with the observation-level spectral records.

We also create an observation identifier (ObsID) combining environment and genotype. This is useful because the same genotype can appear in more than one environment, and unique observation labels greatly improve traceability and alignment checks.

The code below assembles the phenotypic table that serves as the observation-level backbone for the aligned matrices.

Pheno <- NIR %>%
  dplyr::select(Pedigree, Env, GY, KW) %>%
  arrange(Env, Pedigree) %>%
  mutate(ObsID = paste(Env, Pedigree, sep = "__"))

head(Pheno)

4. Harmonize genotype IDs across data sources

This is a critical validation step. The genomic file and the NIR/phenotypic file must refer to the same genotypes. If the IDs are not harmonized before matrix construction, every downstream kernel will be misaligned.

The code below harmonizes genotype identifiers across data sources so that genomic, phenomic, and phenotypic information refer to the same materials.

Pedigree <- intersect(Geno$taxa, NIR$Pedigree)

NIR <- NIR[NIR$Pedigree %in% Pedigree, ] %>%
  arrange(Env, Pedigree) %>%
  mutate(ObsID = paste(Env, Pedigree, sep = "__"))

Geno <- Geno[Geno$taxa %in% Pedigree, ] %>%
  arrange(taxa)

Pheno <- Pheno[Pheno$Pedigree %in% Pedigree, ] %>%
  arrange(Env, Pedigree) %>%
  mutate(ObsID = paste(Env, Pedigree, sep = "__"))

cat("Shared genotypes in NIR :", length(unique(NIR$Pedigree)), "\n")
cat("Shared genotypes in Geno:", nrow(Geno), "\n")
cat("Shared genotypes in Pheno:", length(unique(Pheno$Pedigree)), "\n")

5. Split NIR observations by environment

The study has four environments:

  • CS11_WS
  • CS11_WW
  • CS12_WS
  • CS12_WW

The kernel construction later uses environment-specific blocks. For that reason, we separate the NIR observations into four environment-specific data frames while preserving the observation identifiers.

The code below organizes the harmonized data by environment to support later checks of balance, overlap, and kernel construction.

a <- NIR %>% filter(Env == "CS11_WS") %>% arrange(Pedigree)
b <- NIR %>% filter(Env == "CS11_WW") %>% arrange(Pedigree)
c <- NIR %>% filter(Env == "CS12_WS") %>% arrange(Pedigree)
d <- NIR %>% filter(Env == "CS12_WW") %>% arrange(Pedigree)

rownames(a) <- a$ObsID
rownames(b) <- b$ObsID
rownames(c) <- c$ObsID
rownames(d) <- d$ObsID

5.1 Identify genotypes observed in all environments

This object is informative for balanced-data checks and for interpreting the overlap across trials.

The code below identifies the genotypes shared across environments, which is important for stable cross-environment alignment.

Pedigree_communs <- Reduce(
  intersect,
  list(Geno$taxa, a$Pedigree, b$Pedigree, c$Pedigree, d$Pedigree)
)

length(Pedigree_communs)

6. Build the linear genomic kernel (K_G stored as ZG)

The genomic kernel measures similarity among observations based on SNP markers.

Marker values are standardized within each environment-specific observation block before the blocks are combined. The resulting matrix preserves the observation order while placing marker columns on a comparable scale within each environment. This blockwise standardization is part of the kernel definition and must be applied consistently when reproducing the calculation.

The practical steps are:

  1. Sort the genotype matrix and remove the ID column.
  2. Subset the genotype matrix according to the genotypes observed in each environment.
  3. Standardize markers within each block.
  4. Stack the environment-specific matrices in the same order used by the observation table.
  5. Compute the linear kernel by the cross-product of the standardized marker matrix.

The code below constructs the linear genomic kernel used as the basic genomic relationship representation in the pipeline.

Geno <- Geno[order(Geno$taxa), ]
rownames(Geno) <- Geno$taxa
Geno <- Geno[, -1, drop = FALSE]

Geno.a <- scale(Geno[a$Pedigree, , drop = FALSE], center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)
Geno.b <- scale(Geno[b$Pedigree, , drop = FALSE], center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)
Geno.c <- scale(Geno[c$Pedigree, , drop = FALSE], center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)
Geno.d <- scale(Geno[d$Pedigree, , drop = FALSE], center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)

rownames(Geno.a) <- a$ObsID
rownames(Geno.b) <- b$ObsID
rownames(Geno.c) <- c$ObsID
rownames(Geno.d) <- d$ObsID

Geno.all <- rbind(Geno.a, Geno.b, Geno.c, Geno.d)

ZG <- tcrossprod(as.matrix(Geno.all)) / ncol(Geno.all)
rownames(ZG) <- rownames(Geno.all)
colnames(ZG) <- rownames(Geno.all)

dim(ZG)
ZG[1:5, 1:5]

7. Build the linear phenomic kernel (K_P stored as ZP)

The phenomic kernel uses the NIR spectral bands. The same blockwise logic is used here to keep the observation order synchronized with the genomic, categorical environment, and weather kernels.

This kernel is useful because it summarizes spectral similarity among observations. At the same time, it should be interpreted with caution. NIR spectra can reflect strong physiological responses to the field conditions, so the phenomic kernel may capture part of the environmental signal in addition to genotype-related information.

The code below constructs the linear phenomic kernel from the NIR information so it can be compared with the genomic and weather-based structures.

# Identify spectral variables: keep only numeric columns and remove
# metadata/trait columns that should not enter the phenomic kernel.
metadata_cols <- c("Pedigree", "Env", "GY", "KW", "ObsID")

nir_feature_cols <- setdiff(colnames(NIR), metadata_cols)
nir_feature_cols <- nir_feature_cols[
  vapply(NIR[, nir_feature_cols, drop = FALSE], is.numeric, logical(1))
]


excluded_non_numeric <- setdiff(setdiff(colnames(NIR), metadata_cols), nir_feature_cols)

cat("Number of NIR feature columns used in ZP:", length(nir_feature_cols), "\n")
cat("Excluded non-numeric columns:", paste(excluded_non_numeric, collapse = ", "), "\n")

NIR.a <- scale(as.matrix(a[, nir_feature_cols, drop = FALSE]), center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)
NIR.b <- scale(as.matrix(b[, nir_feature_cols, drop = FALSE]), center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)
NIR.c <- scale(as.matrix(c[, nir_feature_cols, drop = FALSE]), center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)
NIR.d <- scale(as.matrix(d[, nir_feature_cols, drop = FALSE]), center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)

rownames(NIR.a) <- a$ObsID
rownames(NIR.b) <- b$ObsID
rownames(NIR.c) <- c$ObsID
rownames(NIR.d) <- d$ObsID

NIR.all <- rbind(NIR.a, NIR.b, NIR.c, NIR.d)

ZP <- tcrossprod(NIR.all) / ncol(NIR.all)
rownames(ZP) <- rownames(NIR.all)
colnames(ZP) <- rownames(NIR.all)

dim(ZP)
ZP[1:5, 1:5]

8. Build the categorical environmental kernel (K_E)

The simplest environmental representation treats each environment as a categorical factor. We first build the incidence matrix ZE, then convert it into a kernel (ZEZE) through the cross-product.

This kernel assigns full environmental similarity to observations from the same environment and zero similarity to observations from different environments. In a prediction model, ZEZE represents the categorical environment effect. It is not the same as the weather kernel. In practical terms, ZEZE keeps the distinction among the four macro-environments, whereas ZW later describes similarity among observations based on meteorological covariates.

The code below builds the categorical environment matrix that represents the macro-environment effect separately from the weather-derived kernel.

Pheno <- Pheno %>% arrange(match(ObsID, rownames(ZG)))

ZE <- model.matrix(~ as.factor(Pheno$Env) - 1)
rownames(ZE) <- Pheno$ObsID
colnames(ZE) <- gsub("^as.factor\\(Pheno\\$Env\\)", "", colnames(ZE))

ZEZE <- tcrossprod(ZE)
rownames(ZEZE) <- rownames(ZE)
colnames(ZEZE) <- rownames(ZE)

cat("Dimensions of ZE   :", dim(ZE), "\n")
cat("Dimensions of ZEZE :", dim(ZEZE), "\n")

ZEZE[1:5, 1:5]

9. Build the weather kernel (K_W stored as ZW)

The weather kernel uses the climate-derived files available in output/climate_results/. Two files are required:

  1. environmental_covariates.csv: one row per year with aggregated weather summaries.
  2. environmental_covariates_expanded.csv: daily records linked to those annual summaries.

This script uses both files:

  • The annual file provides the weather-derived summary covariates.
  • The expanded file provides the daily weather trajectories used to describe the within-season weather signature of each year.

ZW represents similarity derived from meteorological covariates. In models Eta10-Eta18, it can be combined with genomic and phenomic information through interaction kernels such as GGKW, PGKW, GAKW, and PAKW. This representation complements rather than replaces the categorical environment kernel ZEZE.

9.1 Load climate files

The code below reads the climate-derived files required to construct the weather kernel.

annual_ec_file <- "output/climate_results/environmental_covariates.csv"
expanded_ec_file <- "output/climate_results/environmental_covariates_expanded.csv"

ECs_annual <- read.csv(annual_ec_file)
ECs_expanded <- read.csv(expanded_ec_file)

annual_cols <- c(
  "TMAX_AVG", "TMIN_AVG", "GDD_CUM", "PTR_AVG", "HEAT_STRESS_DAYS",
  "PRECTOT", "DRY_DAYS", "RH_AVG", "WS2M_AVG", "RAD_CUM",
  "T_AVG", "DTR_AVG", "COLD_STRESS_DAYS", "RAINY_DAYS",
  "PREC_INTENSITY", "LOW_RH_DAYS", "VPD_AVG", "VPD_STRESS_DAYS"
)

# Keep only true daily weather variables. Exclude identifiers, date encodings,
# coordinate fields, and any annual summary columns that may also be present in
# the expanded file.

  "year", "Year", "YEAR",
  "date", "Date",
  "YYYYMMDD", "MMDD", "DOY",
  "month", "Month", "MM",
  "day", "DD",
  "LON", "LAT"
)

  colnames(ECs_expanded),
  c(daily_id_cols, annual_cols)
)

  vapply(ECs_expanded[, candidate_daily_cols, drop = FALSE], is.numeric, logical(1))
]

  mutate(year = as.integer(year)) %>%
  dplyr::select(year, all_of(annual_cols))

  mutate(
    year = as.integer(year),
    date = as.character(date)
  )

9.2 Standardize annual summaries and daily trajectories

The purpose of this step is to place heterogeneous environmental variables on a comparable scale before kernel construction.

The code below standardizes the climate covariates so that the weather variables are on a comparable scale before kernel construction.

annual_scaled <- ECs_annual %>%
  dplyr::select(all_of(annual_cols)) %>%
  scale(center = TRUE, scale = TRUE) %>%
  as.data.frame()

annual_scaled <- bind_cols(
  year = ECs_annual$year,
  annual_scaled
)

daily_scaled <- ECs_expanded %>%
  dplyr::select(year, date, all_of(daily_cols)) %>%
  group_by(year) %>%
  mutate(across(all_of(daily_cols), ~ as.numeric(scale(.x, center = TRUE, scale = TRUE)))) %>%
  ungroup()

9.3 Create year-specific climatic signatures

For each year, we transform the daily weather trajectories into a single wide-row signature. This gives each year a reproducible vector representing its within-season weather profile.

The code below derives the year-level weather signatures that summarize the standardized climate information for kernel building.

daily_signature_2011 <- daily_scaled %>%
  filter(year == 2011) %>%
  dplyr::select(date, all_of(daily_cols)) %>%
  pivot_longer(cols = -date, names_to = "variable", values_to = "value") %>%
  mutate(signature_col = paste(variable, date, sep = "__")) %>%
  dplyr::select(signature_col, value) %>%
  pivot_wider(names_from = signature_col, values_from = value)

daily_signature_2012 <- daily_scaled %>%
  filter(year == 2012) %>%
  dplyr::select(date, all_of(daily_cols)) %>%
  pivot_longer(cols = -date, names_to = "variable", values_to = "value") %>%
  mutate(signature_col = paste(variable, date, sep = "__")) %>%
  dplyr::select(signature_col, value) %>%
  pivot_wider(names_from = signature_col, values_from = value)

annual_signature_2011 <- annual_scaled %>%
  filter(year == 2011) %>%
  dplyr::select(-year)

annual_signature_2012 <- annual_scaled %>%
  filter(year == 2012) %>%
  dplyr::select(-year)

9.4 Build the observation-level W matrix

Each observation inherits the climatic signature of the year associated with its environment. The 2011 environments receive the 2011 daily and annual signatures; the 2012 environments receive the 2012 signatures. Missing year-specific blocks are filled with NA and later replaced with zero so that the final matrix can be used in a cross-product.

The code below constructs the weather kernel W, which complements rather than replaces the categorical environment structure.

ids_a <- a$ObsID
ids_b <- b$ObsID
ids_c <- c$ObsID
ids_d <- d$ObsID

# Environments from 2011 receive the 2011 daily and annual signatures.
ECs.a_main_daily <- as.data.frame(daily_signature_2011[rep(1, length(ids_a)), , drop = FALSE])
rownames(ECs.a_main_daily) <- ids_a

ECs.a_other_daily <- as.data.frame(
  matrix(
    NA_real_,
    nrow = length(ids_a),
    ncol = ncol(daily_signature_2012),
    dimnames = list(ids_a, colnames(daily_signature_2012))
  )
)

ECs.a_annual <- as.data.frame(annual_signature_2011[rep(1, length(ids_a)), , drop = FALSE])
rownames(ECs.a_annual) <- ids_a

ECs.a <- cbind(ECs.a_main_daily, ECs.a_other_daily, ECs.a_annual)
ECs.a <- as.data.frame(ECs.a)
rownames(ECs.a) <- ids_a

ECs.b_main_daily <- as.data.frame(daily_signature_2011[rep(1, length(ids_b)), , drop = FALSE])
rownames(ECs.b_main_daily) <- ids_b

ECs.b_other_daily <- as.data.frame(
  matrix(
    NA_real_,
    nrow = length(ids_b),
    ncol = ncol(daily_signature_2012),
    dimnames = list(ids_b, colnames(daily_signature_2012))
  )
)

ECs.b_annual <- as.data.frame(annual_signature_2011[rep(1, length(ids_b)), , drop = FALSE])
rownames(ECs.b_annual) <- ids_b

ECs.b <- cbind(ECs.b_main_daily, ECs.b_other_daily, ECs.b_annual)
ECs.b <- as.data.frame(ECs.b)
rownames(ECs.b) <- ids_b

# Environments from 2012 receive the 2012 daily and annual signatures.
ECs.c_main_daily <- as.data.frame(daily_signature_2012[rep(1, length(ids_c)), , drop = FALSE])
rownames(ECs.c_main_daily) <- ids_c

ECs.c_other_daily <- as.data.frame(
  matrix(
    NA_real_,
    nrow = length(ids_c),
    ncol = ncol(daily_signature_2011),
    dimnames = list(ids_c, colnames(daily_signature_2011))
  )
)

ECs.c_annual <- as.data.frame(annual_signature_2012[rep(1, length(ids_c)), , drop = FALSE])

11. Build interaction kernels

The interaction kernels are obtained through the Hadamard product (element-wise multiplication). This operation restricts genomic or phenomic similarity to the environmental context represented by ZEZE or ZW.

The code below constructs the linear interaction kernels that link genomic or phenomic information with environment and weather structures.

ZGZE <- ZG * ZEZE
ZPZE <- ZP * ZEZE
ZGZW <- ZG * ZW
ZPZW <- ZP * ZW

ZGZE[1:5, 1:5]
ZPZE[1:5, 1:5]
ZGZW[1:5, 1:5]
ZPZW[1:5, 1:5]

saveRDS(ZGZE, "output/Matrizes/ZGZE.rds")
saveRDS(ZPZE, "output/Matrizes/ZPZE.rds")

12. Build nonlinear Gaussian kernels

The linear kernels above capture additive similarity. To explore nonlinear relationships, we also build Gaussian kernels for the genomic and phenomic spaces.

12.1 Core mathematical function for the Gaussian kernel

The Gaussian kernel converts pairwise distances into similarities through a bandwidth parameter: larger scaled distances correspond to smaller kernel values. The following marginal-likelihood criterion is used to estimate this parameter for genomic and phenomic distances.

margh.fun <- function(theta, y, D, q, nu = 0.0001, Sc = 0.0001,
                      nuh = NULL, Sch = NULL, prior = NULL) {
  h <- theta[1]
  phi <- theta[2]

  Kh <- exp(-h * D / q)
  eigenKh <- eigen(Kh)

  nr <- length(which(eigenKh$values > 1e-10))
  Uh <- eigenKh$vectors[, 1:nr, drop = FALSE]
  Sh <- eigenKh$values[1:nr]

  d <- t(Uh) %*% scale(y, scale = FALSE)

  lden <- -0.5 * sum(log(1 + phi * Sh)) -
    (nu + nr - 1) / 2 * log(Sc + sum(d^2 / (1 + phi * Sh)))

  if (!is.null(prior)) {
    lprior <- dgamma(h, nuh, Sch, log = TRUE)
  } else {
    lprior <- 0
  }

  return(-(lden + lprior))
}

12.2 Genomic Gaussian kernel (GGK)

We first compute genomic distances among unique genotypes, then project them into the observation space.

The code below computes candidate genomic Gaussian-kernel values before the final bandwidth is fixed.

DG <- (as.matrix(dist(Geno)))^2
q05G <- quantile(DG, 0.05)

IDs <- as.character(Pheno$Pedigree)
DG <- DG[rownames(DG) %in% IDs, rownames(DG) %in% IDs, drop = FALSE]

IDs <- factor(IDs, levels = rownames(DG))
Z <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs - 1))
rownames(Z) <- Pheno$ObsID

ZD <- tcrossprod(Z, DG)
DG_proj <- tcrossprod(ZD, Z)

rownames(DG_proj) <- Pheno$ObsID
colnames(DG_proj) <- Pheno$ObsID

The optimization estimates the genomic Gaussian-kernel parameters under the bounds specified in optim(). These estimates determine how genomic distances are converted to similarities.

solG <- optim(
  c(1, 1),
  margh.fun,
  y = Pheno$GY,
  D = DG_proj,
  q = q05G,
  method = "L-BFGS-B",
  lower = c(0.05, 0.05),
  upper = c(6, 30)
)
saveRDS(solG, "output/Matrizes/solG_GY.RDS")

The code below reads and inspects the selected genomic Gaussian-kernel solution so the final object can be built explicitly.

The code below constructs the final genomic Gaussian kernel using the selected parameter value.

hG <- solG$par[1]

GGK <- exp(-hG * DG_proj / q05G)
rownames(GGK) <- Pheno$ObsID
colnames(GGK) <- Pheno$ObsID

dim(GGK)
GGK[1:5, 1:5]

12.3 Phenomic Gaussian kernel (PGK)

The phenomic Gaussian kernel uses Euclidean distances derived from the standardized NIR matrix.

For GY, the marginal-likelihood calculation uses Pheno$GY to select the phenomic Gaussian bandwidth. The corresponding KW calibration uses Pheno$KW, as described in Section 13.5. The two kernels are stored separately.

The code below computes candidate phenomic Gaussian-kernel values before the final bandwidth is selected.

DP <- (as.matrix(dist(NIR.all)))^2
q05P <- quantile(DP, 0.05)

IDs <- rownames(NIR.all)
DP <- DP[rownames(DP) %in% IDs, rownames(DP) %in% IDs, drop = FALSE]

Z <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs - 1))
rownames(Z) <- IDs

Z0 <- tcrossprod(Z, Z)
ZD <- tcrossprod(Z0, DP)
DP_proj <- tcrossprod(ZD, Z0)

rownames(DP_proj) <- IDs
colnames(DP_proj) <- IDs

The search below identifies the phenomic Gaussian-kernel parameter used to define the final phenomic nonlinear kernel.

solP <- optim(
  c(1, 1),
  margh.fun,
  y = Pheno$GY,
  D = DP_proj,
  q = q05P,
  method = "L-BFGS-B",
  lower = c(0.05, 0.05),
  upper = c(6, 30)
)
saveRDS(solP, "output/Matrizes/solP_GY.RDS")

The code below reads and checks the selected phenomic Gaussian-kernel solution before final construction.

The code below builds the final phenomic Gaussian kernel with the chosen bandwidth.

hP <- solP$par[1]

PGK <- exp(-hP * DP_proj / q05P)
rownames(PGK) <- rownames(NIR.all)
colnames(PGK) <- rownames(NIR.all)

dim(PGK)
PGK[1:5, 1:5]

12.4 Gaussian interaction kernels

The code below generates the Gaussian-kernel interaction structures so the nonlinear kernels can also be combined with environment and weather information.

GGKE <- GGK * ZEZE
PGKE <- PGK * ZEZE
GGKW <- GGK * ZW
PGKW <- PGK * ZW

13. Build nonlinear arccosine kernels

Arccosine kernels offer a second nonlinear representation. They are inspired by neural-network geometry and can capture hierarchical nonlinear patterns through recursive transformations.

The arccosine transformation is applied recursively to describe nonlinear similarity at successive depths. The functions below compute this transformation and evaluate candidate depths for kernel selection.

13.1 Core mathematical functions for arccosine kernels

The functions below define the arccosine kernels used to compute the additive nonlinear structures.

AK1.fun <- function(X) {
  cosalfa <- cor(t(X))
  cosalfa[cosalfa > 1] <- 1
  cosalfa[cosalfa < -1] <- -1
  angulo <- acos(cosalfa)
  mag <- sqrt(apply(X, 1, function(x) crossprod(x)))
  sxy <- tcrossprod(mag)
  AK1 <- (1 / pi) * sxy * (sin(angulo) + (pi - angulo) * cosalfa)
  AK1 <- AK1 / median(AK1, na.rm = TRUE)
  colnames(AK1) <- rownames(X)
  rownames(AK1) <- rownames(X)
  return(AK1)
}

marg.AK <- function(y, AK1, ml) {
  lden.fun <- function(phi, nr, Uh, Sh, d) {
    lden <- -0.5 * sum(log(1 + phi * Sh)) -
      (nr - 1) / 2 * log(sum(d^2 / (1 + phi * Sh)))
    return(-lden)
  }

  vero <- function(y, GC) {
    Kh <- GC
    eigenKh <- eigen(Kh)
    nr <- length(which(eigenKh$values > 1e-10))
    Uh <- eigenKh$vectors[, 1:nr, drop = FALSE]
    Sh <- eigenKh$values[1:nr]
    d <- t(Uh) %*% scale(y, scale = FALSE)
    sol <- optimize(lden.fun, nr = nr, Uh = Uh, Sh = Sh, d = d,
                    lower = 0.0005, upper = 200)
    phi <- sol[[1]]
    log.vero <- 0.5 * sum(log(1 + phi * Sh)) -
      (nr - 1) / 2 * log(sum(d^2 / (1 + phi * Sh)))
    return(log.vero)
  }

  GC <- AK1
  l <- 1
  GC2 <- GC
  vero1 <- vero(y = y, GC = GC2)
  m <- 0

  while (m == 0 && (l < ml)) {
    l <- l + 1
    GC <- AK.fun(AK1 = GC2, nl = 1)
    GC2 <- GC
    vero2 <- vero(y = y, GC = GC2)
    if (vero2 < vero1) m <- 1
    vero1 <- vero2
  }

  return(l - 1)
}

AK.fun <- function(AK1, nl) {
  n <- nrow(AK1)
  AK <- AK1
  for (l in 1:nl) {
    Aux <- tcrossprod(diag(AK))
    cosalfa <- AK * (Aux^(-1 / 2))
    cosa <- as.vector(cosalfa)
    cosa[cosa > 1] <- 1
    cosa[cosa < -1] <- -1
    angulo <- acos(cosa)
    angulo <- matrix(angulo, n, n)
    AK <- (1 / pi) * (Aux^(1 / 2)) * (sin(angulo) + (pi - angulo) * cos(angulo))
  }
  AK <- AK / median(AK, na.rm = TRUE)
  rownames(AK) <- rownames(AK1)
  colnames(AK) <- colnames(AK1)
  return(AK)
}

13.2 Genomic arccosine kernel (GAK)

For GY, the recursion depth is selected from the marginal criterion evaluated with Pheno$GY. The corresponding genomic arccosine kernel for KW is calibrated separately, as described in Section 13.5.

The code below constructs the genomic arccosine kernel candidate used in the nonlinear genomic pathway.

GAK1 <- AK1.fun(Geno)
dim(GAK1)
GAK1[1:5, 1:5]

IDs <- as.character(Pheno$Pedigree)
GAK1 <- GAK1[rownames(GAK1) %in% IDs, rownames(GAK1) %in% IDs, drop = FALSE]

IDs <- factor(IDs, levels = rownames(GAK1))
Z <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs - 1))
rownames(Z) <- Pheno$ObsID
G <- tcrossprod(tcrossprod(Z, GAK1), Z)

Gl <- marg.AK(y = Pheno$GY, AK1 = G, ml = 50)

IDs.L <- factor(Pheno$Pedigree, levels = rownames(GAK1))
Z.L <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs.L - 1))
rownames(Z.L) <- Pheno$ObsID
G.L <- tcrossprod(tcrossprod(Z.L, GAK1), Z.L)

GAK <- AK.fun(AK1 = G.L, nl = Gl)
rownames(GAK) <- Pheno$ObsID
colnames(GAK) <- Pheno$ObsID

13.3 Phenomic arccosine kernel (PAK)

The phenomic arccosine recursion depth is estimated with Pheno$GY for GY and with Pheno$KW for KW. Even when the selected values coincide, the matrices are identified by trait.

The code below constructs the phenomic arccosine kernel candidate used in the nonlinear phenomic pathway.

PAK1 <- AK1.fun(NIR.all)
dim(PAK1)
PAK1[1:5, 1:5]

IDs <- rownames(NIR.all)
PAK1 <- PAK1[rownames(PAK1) %in% IDs, rownames(PAK1) %in% IDs, drop = FALSE]

Z <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs - 1))
rownames(Z) <- IDs
Z0 <- tcrossprod(Z, Z)
ZD <- tcrossprod(Z0, PAK1)
P <- tcrossprod(ZD, Z0)

Pl <- marg.AK(y = Pheno$GY, AK1 = P, ml = 50)

IDs.L <- rownames(NIR.all)
Z.L <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs.L - 1))
rownames(Z.L) <- IDs.L
Z0.L <- tcrossprod(Z.L, Z.L)
P.L <- tcrossprod(tcrossprod(Z0.L, PAK1), Z0.L)

PAK <- AK.fun(AK1 = P.L, nl = Pl)
rownames(PAK) <- rownames(NIR.all)
colnames(PAK) <- rownames(NIR.all)

The checks below confirm that the phenomic arccosine kernel is coherent.

13.4 Arccosine interaction kernels

The code below builds the interaction kernels associated with the arccosine structures so they can be used in the downstream models.

GAKE <- GAK * ZEZE
PAKE <- PAK * ZEZE
GAKW <- GAK * ZW
PAKW <- PAK * ZW

GAKE[1:5, 1:5]
PAKE[1:5, 1:5]
GAKW[1:5, 1:5]
PAKW[1:5, 1:5]

13.5 One-time trait-specific calibration for kernel weight (KW)

The matrices above reproduce the existing GY-calibrated nonlinear kernels. To construct the corresponding KW-calibrated Gaussian and arccosine kernels, the following chunk uses the same distances, parameter bounds, marginal-likelihood functions, and recursion rules, replacing only the response by Pheno$KW. It reads the saved inputs and writes separate _KW outputs, without repeating the GY calibration or the BGLR prediction stage. The chunk is disabled for regular rendering; it is executed explicitly once during the KW calibration step.

# One-time KW calibration. The Gaussian and arccosine functions are
# defined above; only the KW-dependent parameters are estimated here.
Pheno_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/Pheno.rds")
NIR_KW <- as.matrix(readRDS("output/Matrizes/NIR.all.rds"))
ZEZE_KW <- as.matrix(readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZEZE.rds"))
ZW_KW <- as.matrix(readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZW.rds"))

Geno_KW <- as.data.frame(data.table::fread(
  "data/GAPIT.Genotype.Numerical.txt"
))
Geno_KW <- Geno_KW[Geno_KW$taxa %in% Pheno_KW$Pedigree, , drop = FALSE]
Geno_KW <- Geno_KW[order(Geno_KW$taxa), , drop = FALSE]
rownames(Geno_KW) <- Geno_KW$taxa
Geno_KW <- as.matrix(Geno_KW[, -1, drop = FALSE])

# Gaussian genomic kernel: same distance and marginal criterion as GY.
DG_KW <- (as.matrix(dist(Geno_KW)))^2
q05G_KW <- quantile(DG_KW, 0.05)
IDs_KW <- factor(
  as.character(Pheno_KW$Pedigree),
  levels = rownames(DG_KW)
)
Z_KW <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs_KW - 1))
rownames(Z_KW) <- Pheno_KW$ObsID
DG_KW <- DG_KW[levels(IDs_KW), levels(IDs_KW), drop = FALSE]
DG_proj_KW <- Z_KW %*% DG_KW %*% t(Z_KW)

solG_KW <- optim(
  c(1, 1), margh.fun,
  y = Pheno_KW$KW, D = DG_proj_KW, q = q05G_KW,
  method = "L-BFGS-B",
  lower = c(0.05, 0.05), upper = c(6, 30)
)
GGK_KW <- exp(-solG_KW$par[1] * DG_proj_KW / q05G_KW)

# Gaussian phenomic kernel: NIR observations remain in Pheno order.
NIR_KW <- NIR_KW[Pheno_KW$ObsID, , drop = FALSE]
DP_KW <- (as.matrix(dist(NIR_KW)))^2
q05P_KW <- quantile(DP_KW, 0.05)
solP_KW <- optim(
  c(1, 1), margh.fun,
  y = Pheno_KW$KW, D = DP_KW, q = q05P_KW,
  method = "L-BFGS-B",
  lower = c(0.05, 0.05), upper = c(6, 30)
)
PGK_KW <- exp(-solP_KW$par[1] * DP_KW / q05P_KW)

# Arccosine genomic kernel: estimate recursion depth for KW.
GAK1_KW <- AK1.fun(Geno_KW)
ZAK_KW <- as.matrix(model.matrix(~ IDs_KW - 1))
rownames(ZAK_KW) <- Pheno_KW$ObsID
GAK1_proj_KW <- ZAK_KW %*%
  GAK1_KW[levels(IDs_KW), levels(IDs_KW), drop = FALSE] %*%
  t(ZAK_KW)
Gl_KW <- marg.AK(y = Pheno_KW$KW, AK1 = GAK1_proj_KW, ml = 50)
GAK_KW <- if (Gl_KW == 0) {
  GAK1_proj_KW
} else {
  AK.fun(AK1 = GAK1_proj_KW, nl = Gl_KW)
}

# Arccosine phenomic kernel: estimate recursion depth for KW.
PAK1_KW <- AK1.fun(NIR_KW)
Pl_KW <- marg.AK(y = Pheno_KW$KW, AK1 = PAK1_KW, ml = 50)
PAK_KW <- if (Pl_KW == 0) {
  PAK1_KW
} else {
  AK.fun(AK1 = PAK1_KW, nl = Pl_KW)
}

# Project all KW kernels to the shared observation index.
kw_ids <- as.character(Pheno_KW$ObsID)
for (name in c("GGK_KW", "PGK_KW", "GAK_KW", "PAK_KW")) {
  K <- get(name)
  rownames(K) <- kw_ids
  colnames(K) <- kw_ids
  assign(name, K)
}
ZEZE_KW <- ZEZE_KW[kw_ids, kw_ids, drop = FALSE]
ZW_KW <- ZW_KW[kw_ids, kw_ids, drop = FALSE]

GGKE_KW <- GGK_KW * ZEZE_KW
PGKE_KW <- PGK_KW * ZEZE_KW
GGKW_KW <- GGK_KW * ZW_KW
PGKW_KW <- PGK_KW * ZW_KW
GAKE_KW <- GAK_KW * ZEZE_KW
PAKE_KW <- PAK_KW * ZEZE_KW
GAKW_KW <- GAK_KW * ZW_KW
PAKW_KW <- PAK_KW * ZW_KW

# Persist only KW parameters and matrices. GY objects are untouched.
kw_names <- c(
  "solG_KW", "solP_KW", "Gl_KW", "Pl_KW",
  "GGK_KW", "PGK_KW", "GAK_KW", "PAK_KW",
  "GGKE_KW", "PGKE_KW", "GGKW_KW", "PGKW_KW",
  "GAKE_KW", "PAKE_KW", "GAKW_KW", "PAKW_KW"
)
for (name in kw_names) {
  suffix <- if (name %in% c("solG_KW", "solP_KW")) ".RDS" else ".rds"
  saveRDS(get(name), file.path("output/Matrizes", paste0(name, suffix)))
}
print(data.frame(
  Trait = "KW",
  GenomicGaussianH = solG_KW$par[1],
  PhenomicGaussianH = solP_KW$par[1],
  GenomicArccosineDepth = Gl_KW,
  PhenomicArccosineDepth = Pl_KW
))

Gaussian parameters and KW depths are read from the persisted calibration files. The GY depths (49 for both kernels) are the values documented in the previous manuscript; they were not saved as separate RDS objects. The table reports these historical results without rerunning the search.

solG <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/solG_GY.RDS")
solP <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/solP_GY.RDS")
solG_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/solG_KW.RDS")
solP_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/solP_KW.RDS")
Gl_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/Gl_KW.rds")
Pl_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/Pl_KW.rds")

kernel_parameters <- data.frame(
  Trait = c("GY", "KW"),
  GenomicGaussianH = c(solG$par[1], solG_KW$par[1]),
  PhenomicGaussianH = c(solP$par[1], solP_KW$par[1]),
  GenomicGaussianPhi = c(solG$par[2], solG_KW$par[2]),
  PhenomicGaussianPhi = c(solP$par[2], solP_KW$par[2]),
  GenomicArccosineDepth = c(49L, Gl_KW),
  PhenomicArccosineDepth = c(49L, Pl_KW)
)

14. Summarize nonlinear kernels

The table below provides a final integrity check for the nonlinear kernels before export.

The output below summarizes the nonlinear kernels and their interactions to provide a final diagnostic view of the constructed objects.

kernel_inventory_nonlinear <- data.frame(
  Trait = rep(c("GY", "KW"), each = length(nonlinear_names)),
  Kernel = rep(nonlinear_names, times = 2),
  Object = c(nonlinear_names, paste0(nonlinear_names, "_KW"))
)

kernel_inventory_nonlinear$File <- unname(
  matrix_files[kernel_inventory_nonlinear$Object]
)

kernel_inventory_nonlinear$n_rows <- NA_integer_
kernel_inventory_nonlinear$n_cols <- NA_integer_
kernel_inventory_nonlinear$is_square <- NA
kernel_inventory_nonlinear$is_symmetric <- NA
kernel_inventory_nonlinear$is_finite <- NA
kernel_inventory_nonlinear$aligned_with_reference <- NA

for (i in seq_len(nrow(kernel_inventory_nonlinear))) {
  K <- get(kernel_inventory_nonlinear$Object[i])
  kernel_inventory_nonlinear$n_rows[i] <- nrow(K)
  kernel_inventory_nonlinear$n_cols[i] <- ncol(K)
  kernel_inventory_nonlinear$is_square[i] <- nrow(K) == ncol(K)
  kernel_inventory_nonlinear$is_symmetric[i] <- isSymmetric(K)
  kernel_inventory_nonlinear$is_finite[i] <- all(is.finite(K))
  kernel_inventory_nonlinear$aligned_with_reference[i] <-
    identical(rownames(K), reference_ids) &&
    identical(colnames(K), reference_ids)
}

15. Diagnostic visualization of kernel structure

Heatmaps of the full kernel matrices reveal clusters of similar observations and contrasts between genomic and phenomic representations. To make these patterns easier to inspect, the rows and columns are ordered by hierarchical clustering of the kernel-induced distance:

\[ d_{ij}=\sqrt{K_{ii}+K_{jj}-2K_{ij}} \]

The same clustering order is then applied to the rows and columns of the matrix. This makes it easier to identify block structure, local similarity patterns, and contrasts among the linear, Gaussian, and arccosine kernels.

Because these are square matrices, each heatmap is displayed with coord_fixed() and exported with width = height so that the visual aspect ratio matches the mathematical structure of the matrix.

The heatmaps below show the two shared linear kernels and the eight main nonlinear kernels, identified by trait. Each matrix retains its own clustering order and color scale; the visual comparison is structural, rather than a direct comparison of color intensity across panels.

kernel_plot_info <- data.frame(
  KernelName = c(
    "ZG", "GGK_GY", "GGK_KW", "GAK_GY", "GAK_KW",
    "ZP", "PGK_GY", "PGK_KW", "PAK_GY", "PAK_KW"
  ),
  Object = c(
    "ZG", "GGK", "GGK_KW", "GAK", "GAK_KW",
    "ZP", "PGK", "PGK_KW", "PAK", "PAK_KW"
  ),
  KernelLabel = c(
    "Linear genomic kernel (shared)",
    "Gaussian genomic kernel (GY)",
    "Gaussian genomic kernel (KW)",
    "Arccosine genomic kernel (GY)",
    "Arccosine genomic kernel (KW)",
    "Linear phenomic kernel (shared)",
    "Gaussian phenomic kernel (GY)",
    "Gaussian phenomic kernel (KW)",
    "Arccosine phenomic kernel (GY)",
    "Arccosine phenomic kernel (KW)"
  ),
  stringsAsFactors = FALSE
)

kernel_plot_objects <- list()
dir.create("output/Matrizes/figures", recursive = TRUE, showWarnings = FALSE)

for (i in seq_len(nrow(kernel_plot_info))) {

  current_name <- kernel_plot_info$KernelName[i]
  current_label <- kernel_plot_info$KernelLabel[i]
  current_matrix <- get(kernel_plot_info$Object[i])

  current_matrix <- as.matrix(current_matrix)
  current_matrix <- (current_matrix + t(current_matrix)) / 2

  diag_values <- diag(current_matrix)
  distance_squared <- outer(diag_values, diag_values, "+") - 2 * current_matrix
  distance_squared[distance_squared < 0] <- 0
  distance_matrix <- sqrt(distance_squared)

  hc <- hclust(as.dist(distance_matrix), method = "average")
  ord <- hc$order

  current_matrix_ordered <- current_matrix[ord, ord, drop = FALSE]

  rownames(current_matrix_ordered) <- seq_len(nrow(current_matrix_ordered))
  colnames(current_matrix_ordered) <- seq_len(ncol(current_matrix_ordered))

  current_df <- as.data.frame(as.table(current_matrix_ordered))
  colnames(current_df) <- c("Row", "Column", "Value")
  current_df$Row <- factor(current_df$Row, levels = rev(unique(current_df$Row)))
  current_df$Column <- factor(current_df$Column, levels = unique(current_df$Column))

  current_plot <- ggplot(current_df, aes(x = Column, y = Row, fill = Value)) +
    geom_raster() +
    scale_fill_gradient2(
      low = "#2166AC",
      mid = "white",
      high = "#B2182B",
      midpoint = median(current_df$Value, na.rm = TRUE),
      name = "Kernel value"
    ) +
    coord_fixed() +
    labs(
      title = current_name,
      subtitle = current_label,
      x = "Ordered observations",
      y = "Ordered observations"
    ) +
    theme_minimal(base_size = 16) +
    theme(
      plot.title = element_text(face = "bold", size = 12, hjust = 0.5),
      plot.subtitle = element_text(size = 10, hjust = 0.5),
      axis.title = element_text(face = "bold"),
      axis.text = element_blank(),
      axis.ticks = element_blank(),
      legend.title = element_text(face = "bold"),
      legend.text = element_text(size = 8),
      panel.grid = element_blank()
    )

  kernel_plot_objects[[current_name]] <- current_plot
  print(current_plot)
}

16. Save and reload the kernel matrices

Each .rds file preserves an R object, including its numerical matrix entries and row and column labels. Saving the constructed kernels allows subsequent analyses to read the same objects without recalculating the preceding transformations.

The chunk below documents persistence of the shared and GY objects using the explicit file names defined above. To reproduce construction from raw data, execute the construction and calibration chunks in order, execute matrix-file-names without loading the existing matrices, and save the shared and GY objects before running kw-trait-calibration, which uses those persisted inputs. Persistence of KW kernels and their parameters is documented in Section 13.5. During normal rendering, none of these writing chunks are executed.

gy_and_shared_names <- c(shared_matrix_names, nonlinear_names)

dir.create("output/Matrizes", recursive = TRUE, showWarnings = FALSE)

for (obj_name in gy_and_shared_names) {
  saveRDS(
    get(obj_name),
    file.path("output/Matrizes", matrix_files[[obj_name]])
  )
}

# Reload only the saved objects, using their explicit names.
for (obj_name in gy_and_shared_names) {
  assign(obj_name, readRDS(file.path("output/Matrizes", matrix_files[[obj_name]])))
}

17. Summary

The kernels describe genomic, NIR-based phenomic, categorical environmental, and weather-derived similarities. Their interactions combine these sources through element-wise products. Linear kernels are shared, whereas the 24 nonlinear and interaction kernels are identified by GY or KW. Rendering uses the persisted files, preserving calibrated values and observation ordering for variance-component estimation and genomic prediction.
